title: "Abiogenesis"
---

# Abiogenesis

"Origin of life" redirects here. For non-scientific views on the origins of life, see [Creation myth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creation_myth). For the pre-scientific conception of macro-scale organisms randomly arising from non-living materials, see [Spontaneous generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spontaneous_generation).



Stages in the origin of life process range from the well understood, such as the [habitable Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_habitability) and the abiotic synthesis of simple molecules, to the largely unknown, like the derivation of the [last universal common ancestor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_universal_common_ancestor) (LUCA) with its complex molecular functionalities.

[^cite_note-Walker_Packard_Cody_2017-1]

**Abiogenesis** or the **origin of life** (sometimes called biopoiesis) is the natural process by which [life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life) arises from [non-living matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abiotic_component), such as simple [organic compounds](22203.md). The prevailing scientific [hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothesis) is that the transition from non-living to [living entities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organism) on Earth was not a single event, but a process of increasing complexity involving the formation of a [habitable planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_habitability), the prebiotic synthesis of organic molecules, molecular [self-replication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-replication), [self-assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-assembly), [autocatalysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocatalysis), and the emergence of [cell membranes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_membrane). The transition from non-life to life has not been observed experimentally, but many proposals have been made for different stages of the process.

The study of abiogenesis aims to determine how pre-life [chemical reactions](6271.md) gave rise to life under conditions strikingly different from those on Earth today. It uses tools from [biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biology) and [chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemistry), attempting a synthesis of many sciences. Life functions through the chemistry of [carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon) and water, and builds on four chemical families: [lipids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lipid) for cell membranes, [carbohydrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbohydrate) for chemical energy storage and structural composition, [amino acids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amino_acid) for protein metabolism, and the [nucleic acids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleic_acid) [DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA) and [RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNA) for heredity. A theory of abiogenesis must explain the origins and interactions of these classes of molecules.

Many approaches investigate how self-replicating molecules came into existence. Researchers think that life descends from an [RNA world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNA_world), although other self-replicating and self-catalyzing molecules may have preceded RNA. Other approaches (["metabolism-first" hypotheses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-metabolism)) focus on how [catalysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalysis) on the early Earth might have provided the [precursor molecules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precursor_\(chemistry\)) for self-replication. The 1952 [Miller–Urey experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miller%E2%80%93Urey_experiment) demonstrated that amino acids can be synthesized from [inorganic compounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inorganic_compound) under conditions like [early Earth's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prebiotic_atmosphere). Subsequently, amino acids have been found in meteorites, comets, asteroids, and star-forming regions of space.

While the [last universal common ancestor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_universal_common_ancestor) of all modern organisms (LUCA) existed millions of years after the origin of life, its study can guide research into early universal characteristics. A [genomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genomics) approach has sought to characterize LUCA by identifying the genes shared by [Archaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaea) and [Bacteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacteria), major branches of life. It appears there are 60 proteins common to all life and [355 prokaryotic genes](#Phylogeny_and_LUCA) that trace to LUCA; their functions imply that LUCA was [anaerobic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaerobic_respiration) with the [Wood–Ljungdahl pathway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood%E2%80%93Ljungdahl_pathway), deriving energy by [chemiosmosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemiosmosis), and used DNA, the [genetic code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_code), and [ribosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribosome). Earlier cells might have had a leaky membrane and been powered by a naturally occurring [proton gradient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proton_gradient) near a deep-sea white smoker [hydrothermal vent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrothermal_vent); or, life may have originated inside the continental crust or in water at Earth's surface.

Although Earth is the only place known to harbor life, [astrobiologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrobiology) assume that life exists and came into being by similar processes on other planets. [Geochemical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earliest_known_life_forms) and [fossil evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stromatolite) informs most studies. The [Earth was formed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Earth) at 4.54 [Gya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bya), and the earliest evidence of life on Earth dates from 3.8 Gya from [Western Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dresser_Formation). [Fossil micro-organisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micropaleontology#Microfossils) may have lived in hydrothermal vent precipitates [from Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuvvuagittuq_Greenstone_Belt), soon after [ocean formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_water_on_Earth#History_of_water_on_Earth) during the [Hadean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadean), so the process appears to have been relatively rapid in terms of geological time.

## Overview

Further information: [Astrobiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrobiology)



NASA's 2015 strategy for [astrobiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrobiology) aimed to solve the puzzle of the origin of life – how a fully functioning living system could emerge from non-living components – through research on the prebiotic origin of [life's chemicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biochemistry), both in [space](177602.md) and on [planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet), as well as the functioning of early biomolecules to [catalyse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalysis) reactions and support [inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_code).

[^cite_note-NASA_strategy_2015-2]

[Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life#Definitions) consists of reproduction with (heritable) variations.[^cite_note-3] [NASA](18426568.md) defines life as "a self-sustaining chemical system capable of [Darwinian evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution)."[^cite_note-4] Such a system is complex; the [last universal common ancestor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_universal_common_ancestor) (LUCA), presumably a single-celled organism which lived some 4 billion years ago, already had hundreds of [genes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene) encoded in the [DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA) [genetic code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_code) that is universal today. That in turn implies a suite of cellular machinery including [messenger RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messenger_RNA), [transfer RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_RNA), and [ribosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribosome) to translate the code into [proteins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protein). Those proteins included [enzymes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzyme) to operate its [anaerobic respiration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaerobic_respiration) via the [Wood–Ljungdahl metabolic pathway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood%E2%80%93Ljungdahl_pathway), and a [DNA polymerase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_polymerase) to replicate its genetic material.[^cite_note-Witzany_2016-5][^cite_note-AB-20141208-6]

The challenge for origin of life[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-Pereto-8][^cite_note-9] researchers is to explain how such a complex and tightly interlinked system could develop by evolutionary steps, as at first sight [all its parts are necessary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreducible_complexity) to enable it to function. For example, a cell, whether the LUCA or in a modern organism, copies its DNA with the DNA polymerase enzyme, which is itself produced by translating the DNA polymerase gene in the DNA. Neither the enzyme nor the DNA can be produced without the other.[^cite_note-Weiss_Sousa_Mrnjavac_2016-10] The evolutionary process could have started with molecular [self-replication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-replication), [self-assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-assembly) such as of [cell membranes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_membrane), and [autocatalysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocatalysis) via RNA [ribozymes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribozyme) in an [RNA world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNA_world) environment.[^cite_note-Witzany_2016-5][^cite_note-AB-20141208-6][^cite_note-11] The transition of non-life to life has not been observed experimentally.[^cite_note-12] Some scientists see both [life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life) and the origin of life as aspects of the same process.[^cite_note-Pross_2013-13]

The preconditions to the development of a living cell like the LUCA are known, though disputed in detail: a habitable world is formed with a supply of minerals and liquid water and sources of free energy inflows such as our Sun and geothermal heat. Prebiotic synthesis creates a range of simple organic compounds, which are assembled into polymers such as proteins and RNA. On the other side, the process after the LUCA is readily understood: biological evolution caused the development of a wide range of species with varied forms and biochemical capabilities. However, the derivation of the LUCA from simple components is far from understood.[^cite_note-Walker_Packard_Cody_2017-1]

Although Earth remains the only place where life is known,[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15] the science of [astrobiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrobiology) seeks evidence of life on other planets. The 2015 NASA strategy on the origin of life aimed to solve the puzzle by identifying interactions, intermediary structures and functions, energy sources, and environmental factors that contributed to evolvable macromolecular systems,[^cite_note-NASA_strategy_2015-2] and mapping the chemical landscape of potential primordial informational [polymers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polymer). The advent of such polymers was most likely a critical step in prebiotic chemical evolution.[^cite_note-NASA_strategy_2015-2] Those polymers derived, in turn, from simple [organic compounds](22203.md) such as [nucleobases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleobase), [amino acids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amino_acid), and [sugars](27712.md), likely formed by reactions in the environment.[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-Pereto-8][^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18] A successful theory of the origin of life must explain how all these chemicals came into being.[^cite_note-19]

## Pre-1960s conceptual history

Main article: [History of research into the origin of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_research_into_the_origin_of_life)



The [Miller–Urey experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miller%E2%80%93Urey_experiment) was a synthesis of small organic molecules in a mixture of simple gases in a thermal gradient created by heating (right) and cooling (left) the mixture at the same time, with electrical discharges.

### Spontaneous generation

Main article: [Spontaneous generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spontaneous_generation)

One ancient view of the origin of life, from [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle's_biology) until the 19th century, was of [spontaneous generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spontaneous_generation).[^cite_note-20] This held that "lower" animals such as insects were generated by decaying organic substances, and that life arose by chance.[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-Bernal_1967-22] This was questioned from the 17th century, in works like [Thomas Browne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Thomas_Browne)'s *[Pseudodoxia Epidemica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudodoxia_Epidemica)*.[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24] In 1665, [Robert Hooke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Hooke) published the first drawings of a [microorganism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microorganism). In 1676, [Antonie van Leeuwenhoek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonie_van_Leeuwenhoek) drew and described microorganisms, probably [protozoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protozoa) and [bacteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacteria).[^cite_note-25] Van Leeuwenhoek disagreed with spontaneous generation, and by the 1680s convinced himself, using experiments ranging from sealed and open meat incubation and the close study of insect reproduction, that the theory was incorrect.[^cite_note-26] In 1668 [Francesco Redi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francesco_Redi) showed that no [maggots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maggot) appeared in meat when flies were prevented from laying eggs.[^cite_note-27] By the middle of the 19th century, spontaneous generation was considered disproven.[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29]

### Panspermia

Main article: [Panspermia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panspermia)

Dating back to [Anaxagoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaxagoras) in the 5th century BC, [panspermia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panspermia)[^cite_note-30] is the idea that [life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life) originated elsewhere in the [universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universe) and came to Earth. The modern version of panspermia holds that life may have been distributed to Earth by [meteoroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteoroids), [asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroids), [comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comets)[^cite_note-31] or [planetoids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Solar_System_body).[^cite_note-32] This shifts the origin of life to another heavenly body. The advantage is that life is not required to have formed on each planet it occurs on, but in a more limited set of locations, and then spread about the [galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy) to other star systems.[^cite_note-33] There is some interest in the possibility that [life originated on Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_on_Mars) and later transferred to Earth.[^cite_note-34]

### "A warm little pond": primordial soup

Main articles: [Primordial soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primordial_soup) and [Warm little pond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warm_little_pond)

The idea that life originated from non-living matter in slow stages appeared in [Herbert Spencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Spencer)'s 1864–1867 book *Principles of Biology*, and in [William Turner Thiselton-Dyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Turner_Thiselton-Dyer)'s 1879 paper "On spontaneous generation and evolution". On 1 February 1871 [Charles Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin) wrote about these publications to [Joseph Hooker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Dalton_Hooker), and set out his own speculation that the original spark of life may have been in a "[warm little pond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warm_little_pond), with all sorts of [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia) and phosphoric [salts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_\(chemistry\)),—light, heat, electricity \&c present, that a protein compound was chemically formed". Darwin explained that "at the present day such matter would be instantly devoured or absorbed, which would not have been the case before living creatures were formed."[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37]

[Alexander Oparin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Oparin) in 1924 and [J. B. S. Haldane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._B._S._Haldane) in 1929 proposed that the earliest cells slowly self-organized from a [primordial soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primordial_soup), the [Oparin–Haldane hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primordial_soup).[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] Haldane suggested that the Earth's prebiotic oceans consisted of a "hot dilute soup" in which organic compounds could have formed.[^cite_note-Bernal_1967-22][^cite_note-40] [J. D. Bernal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._D._Bernal) showed that such mechanisms could form most of the necessary molecules for life from inorganic precursors.[^cite_note-41] In 1967, he suggested three "stages": the origin of biological [monomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monomer); the origin of biological [polymers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polymer); and the evolution from molecules to cells.[^cite_note-Martin2003-42][^cite_note-43]

### Miller–Urey experiment

Main article: [Miller–Urey experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miller%E2%80%93Urey_experiment)

In 1952, [Stanley Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_L._Miller) and [Harold Urey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_C._Urey) carried out a chemical experiment to demonstrate how organic molecules could have formed spontaneously from inorganic precursors under [prebiotic conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prebiotic_atmosphere) like those posited by the Oparin–Haldane hypothesis. It passed an electric current through a highly [reducing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redox) (lacking oxygen) mixture of gases—[methane](18582230.md), [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia), and [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_gas), with [water vapor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_vapor)—to form organic monomers such as [amino acids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amino_acid).[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45]

Bernal said of the Miller–Urey experiment that "it is not enough to explain the formation of such molecules, what is necessary, is a physical-chemical explanation of the origins of these molecules that suggests the presence of suitable sources and sinks for free energy."[^cite_note-46] However, current scientific consensus describes the primitive atmosphere as weakly reducing or neutral,[^cite_note-Cleaves_2008-47][^cite_note-48] diminishing the amount and variety of amino acids that could be produced. The addition of [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron) and [carbonate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbonate) minerals, present in early oceans, produces a diverse array of amino acids.[^cite_note-Cleaves_2008-47] Later work has focused on two other potential reducing environments: [outer space](177602.md) and deep-sea hydrothermal vents.[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51]
